E.6. Monitoring and evaluation  
 
Project -level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with FAO policies. The project inception 
phase will include the formulation of a Theory of Change describing the causal relationship between 
outcomes that culminate with achieving the desired change and the underlying assumptions about how the  
change will happen. FAO will ensure a well- designed, operational , and effective impact monitoring and 
measurement system to measure the causal and attributable change, as well as  the contribution and 
overall causal results of the project. The monitoring system should be designed to understand the efficacy  
and targeting and verify the assumptions that the program is making. It should also be used to generate 
information, data and lessons that can be used to feed back into the project  implementation and planning 
components. Progress will be measured against baselines, targets and indicators.  
FAO will perform monitoring and reporting throughout the reporting period. FAO has a country’s  presence and 
capacity to perform these tasks. FAO will implement  the tools and methods to facilitate monitoring of the 
project.  The methods will support vertical monitoring from the beneficiaries to management and facilitate  
comparative and standardized monitoring. The PMU will use the tools , including the workplans platform , to 
monitor activities and develop reports to the Project Board that combine financial reporting and  progress 
toward achieving results set out in the Performance Management Fr amework.  
The monitoring system will be comprehensive and entail the Project workplan as well as the action plans for  
gender, customary landowners and communities, biodiversity , and the social and environmental 
framework.  
The day -to-day project monitoring and implementation responsibility rests on a national ly recruited Project  
Manager , with the support of the rest of the national and international staff,  who will lead the PMU. S/he will 
be supported by a monitoring and evaluation specialist, who will lead the PMU's Monitoring and Evaluation 
Unit. The M&E Specialist will coordinate the annual work plans to ensure  the efficient implementation of the 
project. The Project Manager  will inform the P roject Board and FAO Country Office of any delays or  
difficulties during implementation, including the M& E plan so that appropriate and corrective measures can 
be adopted. The PM will ensure that all project staff maintain high  transparency, responsibility and 
accountability when  monitoring and reporting project results. FAO will support the P roject M anager  as 
needed through annual monitoring missions. Additional M&E and implementation quality assurance and 
troubleshooting support will be provided by FAO as needed. The CCDA, PNGFA, CE PA, other related 
institutions,  project beneficiaries and stakeholders will be involved as much as possible in project -level 
M&E.  
A project inception workshop will be implemented to: a) agree on the project theory of change, b ) build a common 
understanding of  the project strategy and discuss any change in the overall context that might influence 
implementation, c ) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the project team and Project Board, including 
reporting and communication lines , d) review the results framework and discussion, reporting, monitoring 
and evaluation roles and responsibilities, and to finalize the M&E plans , e) review financial reporting 
requirements , and f ) planning and scheduling ESC meetings , and g ) finalize the first year work plan. The 
final Inception Report will be approved by the Project Board  and FAO.  
The PM and FAO will provide input  for the Annual Report for each year of implementation. The PM and the M&E 
Specialist will ensure that the indicators in the results framework are monitored annually. The Annual 
Reports will be shared with the Steering Committee and other stakeholders. The annual performance 
reports will be due to GCF 60 days after the end of the year calendar. The final project annual report and 
the terminal evaluation report will be the final project report package. Mid- term and final evaluations will be 
undertaken under the responsibility of FAO Office of Evaluation (OED) . 
In summary, monitoring and evaluation at the project level will be carried out in accordance with FAO's  corporate 
systems and will be undertaken according to the FAO evaluation policy across the  project life cycle. The


---
*Page 99*
---

 
 
REDD- plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL  
GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 96 OF 9 
 
 
 
Project Cycle establishes institutional standards and procedures for project management,  improving 
accountability and quality through results -based management principles, while  improving the strategic 
focus of all FAO projects.  
The Project Cycle sets corporate standards and procedures, including quality assurance criteria for phases 1 to 3 
of all FAO  ́s projects. These criteria are relevance, feasibility and sustainability , which are complemented 
by the performance assessment during the implementation of the project (Initial Installation, Annual Project  
Report, Mid- term and Final Evaluation).  
The project team and the FAO Country Office will carry out the M&E Plan in accordance with FAO procedures.  
The project document within the Field Programme Management Information System ( FPMIS ) will also 
include additional information, such as the corresponding means of verification. The M&E plan includes an 
initial report, annual reports to the GCF, project implementation reviews, a mid- term and a final  evaluation. 
This information will be available online.  
The following sections describe the main elements of this plan. The project M&E plan will be presented and 
finalized after an adjustment of the indicators, means of verification, and a full definition of the M&E  
responsibilities of the project staff.  
• Initial installation  
The Project Inception Workshop will be held within the first 3 months of the project start, involving those with 
assigned roles in the project organization structure, FAO Country Office and, where appropriate/feasible, 
FAO regional technical policy and technical advisors as well as key stakeholders. The Inception Workshop 
is crucial  to building ownership of the project results and planning the first- year annual work plan.  
• Annual Project Report  
This important report is prepared by the Project Technical Advisors, consolidated by the Project Manager, 
reviewed by the REDD+ TWC and final ly approved by the Project  Board to monitor progress made since 
the project start ed and for the previous reporting period.  
The format and content of the annual report will be adjusted based on the simplified reporting regime established 
for RBP by the GCF.  
• Mid-term evaluation  
The project will undergo an independent mid- term evaluation at the mid- point of project implementation. The mid-
term evaluation will determine progress towards the achievement of outcomes and will suggest corrective 
actions if needed. It will focus on the effectiveness, efficiency , and timeliness of project implementation, 
highlight issues requiring decisions and actions , and present initial lessons learned about project design, 
implementation, and management.  
• Final Evaluation  
An independent Final Evaluation will be launched within six months before the project’s  completion date, NTE. It 
will aim to  identify  project outcomes, their sustainability , and actual or potential impacts, including , inter 
alia, global environmental benefits. It will also indicat e future actions needed to ensure the continuity of the 
process developed through the project. Both mid- term and final evaluations will be managed by FAO OED 
and coordinat e with the FAO Regional Technical Advisor and the Project Board.  
During the final three months, the project team will prepare the Project Terminal Report. This comprehensive 
report will be available to the public through the PNG REDD+  website. It will summarize the results 
achieved (objectives, outcomes, outputs), lessons learned, problems met and areas where results may not 
have been achieved. It will also lay out recommendations for any further steps that may need to be taken 
to ensure the sustainability and replicability of the project’s results  
 
F. Legal arrangements  
E.6.1. Legal title to REDD -plus results  
 
According to Section 78B of the Climate Change (Management) (Amendment) Act (CCMA, n.25/2021) repealed 
by the CCMA , Amendments (n. 17/2023) , if the Government engages in any transaction under international 
REDD+ programmes, including the Green Climate Fund RBPs programme, the “[National Climate Change] 
Board” has the authority of the landholder to sell and transfer all carbon sequestered by the  forest including


---
*Page 100*
---

 
 
REDD- plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL  
GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 97 OF 9 
 
 
 
in the form of emissions reductions, (...) or carbon sequestration resulting from that programme provided 
that:“   
(b) The Government compensates the customary landowners and communities  through payment for 
environmental services (PES), where relevant, and any other landholder with forest carbon 
included in the transaction under an approved benefits sharing plan subject to Section 93.   
(c) the Board has considered the market value of the emissions reductions (…) at the time of evaluating 
the proposed transaction and the impact that such a transaction will have on Papua New Guinea’s 
NDC.    
(d) and any other necessary arrangements with land holders are entered”.   
As it concerns the functions of the Board, Section 2 of the CCMA amendments (n.17/2023) repeals section 13 of 
the CCMA (2015, not repealed) and redirects the specifies that the Board (e) administration ERs and the 
receipt of donor funding (such as results -based finance) from REDD+ activities and other national or 
international climate compatible development Programs from the M inister back to the Board. ". This has 
been succinctly covered within the various amendments stipulated within the preceding provisions of 78B 
(n.25/2021) and section 9 of the CCMA Amendments (n.17/2023).   
To this effect, the Board in consultation with the Minister responsible for forestry matters approves a benefit 
sharing plan for any transaction under international REDD+ programmes.” (Section 78B (2), CCMA 
Amendments n. 25/2021).    
In the context of GCF, no ER transactions will occur . Still, the competent authority has to demonstrate its 
capacity to receive and administer Result -Based Payments (RBPs), in accordance with the legal 
framework and ensure the legitimacy of the process, on the basis of a participatory and inclusive benefit -
sharing plan, the full respect of rights of the parties involved in ERs activities (customary landowners and 
communities  rights, and beneficiaries’ rights), and avoiding any potential claims on the results to be paid by 
the GCF.   
 
Section 3 of the CCMA Amendment (n.25/2021) repeals Section 2 of the principal Act and defines “carbon rights” 
as legal claims, verified through social and land mapping, on the benefit streams from carbon sinks.” 
Further to this, Section 92A stipulates  that carbon rights and ownership. may  be defined and regulated 
under the Act and subsection (2) states that  a Regulation may provide the safeguards, procedures and 
guidelines on (a) how carbon rights are defined;(b) landownership; and c) institutional arrangements, 
considering the principles of natural resource ownership in Papua New Guinea.”   
In Papua New Guinea, customary law is fully recognized by the Constitution of 1975, and under customary law, 
forest resources are owned by the customary owners of the relevant  custo mary  land. In this regard, 
Section 46 of the Forestry Act (1991) states that “the rights of customary owners of forest resources are to 
be fully recognized and respected in all transactions affecting the resource”.   
Section 57 of the CCMA Amendments n.25/2021 amends section 90 to include rights and benefit sharing within 
all climate change project agreements entered into with landowners . This ensures that they have the rights 
to the benefits derived from climate change projects. In addition, Section 59 states that for the purposes of 
Section 1(3) of the Organic Law on Provincial Governments and Local -level Governments (n.28/1998), a CLIMATE CHANGE CARBON MARKET REGULATION  
 
Climate Change Carbon Market regulations is currently before the Governor General for endorsement so may be approved by the time of the submission to this proposal. 
The regulation includes relevant elements, such as:  
 
• It sets out the specifics for issuance of permits for entities entering into carbon 
projects providing a framework for administering as well as determining 
carbon rights via a system of registration that will help to track and monitor as 
well as verify the  accuracy/legitimacy of any claims made or in the event of 
any competing claims that may arise.  
 
• It also gives effect to the CCMA amendments of 2023 in so far as it relates to the framework for Benefit sharing from the sale and transfer of carbon credits including REDD+ transactions.


---
*Page 101*
---

 
 
REDD- plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL  
GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 98 OF 9 
 
 
 
regulation shall prescribe the benefit sharing arrangement of revenues generated by the Authority and 
further define the processes and allowances for the collection of money  for the purpose of Provincial and 
Local -level Governments (where necessary).   
On the other hand, Section 57 (CCMA, 25/2021) further amends S ection 90 of the Principal Act and reiterates 
that: “(1) A climate change related project agreement shall (b) explain and define land holder, rights and 
benefits, including monetary or PES and other benefits, if any, to be received by the land holders, as 
prescribed under Part IX, in consideration for the rights granted, and (g) be accompanied by a stakeholder 
engagement and awareness plan specific to the project area land holders and land holder representatives.   
To that effect, the benefit -sharing plan will specify criteria and modalities to ensure that REDD+ Results -Based 
Payments will be channel ed through appropriate mechanisms and that beneficiaries’ rights, including those 
holding carbon rights , will be respected.   
In this regard, PNG has recently adopted a set of Guidelines on benefit -sharing and distribution (2023) to enable 
the CCDA as the mandated Authority according to the CCMA 2021 (s 10 (d)) to “administer compensation 
or incentive funds, donor money and other  funding derived from national and international climate finance 
under the direction of the Board”(…) in line with relevant sections of the Climate Change (Management)  
Act 2015 (as Amended), the National REDD+ Strategy 2017- 2027 and the REDD+ Finance and 
Investment Prospectus.    
Beneficiaries may include but are not limited to , communities, civil society, and the private sector, including any 
nested REDD+ projects, while national and sub- national governments may also retain a certain number  of 
payments to cover their costs for implementing and/or managing REDD+ implementation.   
Eligibility criteria for beneficiaries should be clearly described in the Benefit S haring Plan (BSP) , avoiding 
excluding relevant or vulnerable stakeholders. They might include:    
• Primary beneficiaries who play a direct role in reducing deforestation and degradation on the ground, 
perform vital monitoring and reporting functions and have a regulatory or legal right to the land, forests or 
emission reductions.    
 
• Secondary beneficiaries who have important roles in the implementation of REDD+ , but their roles and 
impacts are largely indirect.  To ensure that BSPs can be implemented effectively, practical approaches 
outlined in the National REDD+ FPIC Guidelines, must also be considered to overcome any exclusion of 
key stakeholders in Benefit Sharing Agreements (BSA).Due consideration must also be given to the 
mechanisms proposed to  facilitate the sharing and distribution of benefits to REDD+ beneficiaries in a 
timely manner, reflected in the REDD+ BSAs.    
The development of a sound, inclusive and participatory BSP will then avoid excluding vulnerable groups and 
other potential beneficiaries from the programme and limit any potential claim on the GCF results to be 
paid.

Secretariat ’s assessment  of FP273 
Proposal name:  Papua New Guinea  REDD -plus RBP for results period 2014 -  
2016  
Accredited entity : Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations  (FAO)  
Country : Papua New Guinea  
Project  size:  Medium  
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
1.1 Project background  
1. At its eighteenth meeting ( B.18), the Board of the GCF decided to allocate up to USD 500 
million to the request for proposals for the  REDD+ Results -Based Payments Pilot Programme  
(REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme ). At the same meeting, the Board adopted draft terms of 
reference for the REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme  and the corresponding draft scorecard, which 
determines the eligibility of the  results -based payment  (RBP) request and discount to be 
applied to the REDD+ results submitted to GCF for payment. However, the financial envelope 
was exhausted in 2020, with four concept notes ( from the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, 
Papua New Guinea, Uganda and Viet Nam) pending to this day due to the lack of  available funds .  
2. At B .39, the Board decided on an exceptional basis to allow these four legacy REDD+ 
RBP concept notes to be submitted as funding proposals under the  REDD+ RBP Pilot 
Programme , subject to the relevant review and due diligence to be carried out by the Secretariat 
for a funding proposal.  
3. The project prese nts Papua New Guinea’s  REDD-plus results for the period 2014 –2016, 
which calculates that a volume of around 16. 9 million tonnes  of carbon dioxide equivalent (t  CO 2 
eq) in emission reductions  (ERs)  from reducing emissions from deforestation have been offered 
to GCF. The reported emission reductions have undergone technical assessment.  
4. These results are presented to GCF for RBP s under the  REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme  
and are fully compliant with the eligibility criteria set out in the terms of reference (TOR)  for the  
REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme  (decision B.18/07). The presented results, which comply with 
United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) standards and the TOR , 
have been analysed based on the scorecard and are presented in the annex to this assessment.  
5. Papua New Guinea  will use the proceeds from the RBP to support the n ational and 
targeted provincial governments of Papua New Guinea in implementing the National REDD+ 
Strategy for 2017 –2027  (NRS) . This implementation will focus on key action areas, including 
strengthened land planning, law enforcement, forest protection, and the promotion of inclusive sustainable livelihoods for communities . The project has two components:  
Component 1: Increased institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial 
governments in P NG and the targeted provinces ;  
Component 2: Enhanced economic productivity and sustainable livelihoods of smallholders and 
customary landowning groups in the targeted provinces.

II. Assessment of perfo rmance against investment criteria  
2.1 Impact potential                                     Scale:  High  
10. The project is fully aligned with the NRS and offers a unique opportunity for PNG to 
continue its investments and innovations in the forest sector through the  proposed use of 
proceeds . The proposal aims to catalyse transformational change in Papua New Guinea’ s forest 
and Land -use sectors by prioritizing low -emission development. It seeks to reshape land use 
and economic growth towards long -term sustainability  by aligning with Papua New Guinea’ s 
goal of achieving lower emissions, stronger economic growth, an d improved community 
livelihoods. The transformation promotes the sustainable and equitable use of forest resources, 
ensuring benefits for current and future generations.  
11. The proposal addresses drivers of forest loss and degradation through coordinated 
efforts across government, civil society, and private stakeholders. It encourages sustainable 
resource use, integrating REDD+ into decision -making processes to align economic growth with 
environmental and climate goals . 
2.2 Paradigm shift potential                     Scale: High  
12. The project aims to address the challenges of a rapidly growing rural population and 
pressure on land resources by promoting sustainable economic development in targeted 
provinces. It focuses on supporting customary landowners, improving Land -use planning, and 
promoting small -scale forestry to mitigate the effects of agricultural expansion while unlocking 
opportunities for rural communities.  
13. Direct activities will initially focus on six provinces. Successful outcomes will serve as 
replicable models for other regions, supported by capacity -building, policy integration, 
economic incentives and community engagement.  
14. The project aims to reduce deforestation, enhance agricultural productivity, and 
improve livelihoods in the targeted provinces. It offers a pathway for national transformation  by 
addressing environmental and socioeconomic challenges, ensuring sustainable growth, and 
preserving Papua New Guinea’ s environmental heritage for future generations.


---
*Page 110*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 110 
 
2.3 Sustainable development potential                                  Scale:  High  
15. The project aims to tackle the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation 
while offering broader environmental, social and economic benefits. It adopts a holistic 
approach to sustainable development by addressing environmental degradation, social 
inequities, and economic challenges,  in alignment  with national policies for coherence and 
scalability. This contributes to preserving Papua New Guinea’ s environmental assets, 
empowering rural communities, and promoting sustainable economic development.  
16. The project strengthens environmental governance, aligns land use planning with 
conservation principles, and enhances the enforcement of environmental regulations. It 
supports sustainable timber production and conservation efforts, such as integrating 
environmental impact assessments into development plans and promoting community 
stewardship of critical habitats.  
17. Empowering local communities and customary landowners is central, fostering local 
ownership and participation in sustainable land management. The project prioritizes gender equality  by ensuring women ’s active involvement and addressing disparities. Improved 
agricultural systems enhance food security and diversify economic opportunities, supported by strengthened extension services.  
18. The project creates sustainable economic opportunities aligned with international 
standards, supporting local communities and fostering sustainable agriculture. It enhances 
productivity, market access, and rural infrastructure, creating employment and entr epreneurial 
opportunities. The initiative supports institutional coordination, fostering partnerships for a 
transformative, low -emission economic model that benefits all sectors of society.  
2.4 Needs of the recipient                       Scale: High  
19. Papua New Guinea  has experienced rapid economic growth in the  twenty -first  century, 
primarily driven by foreign investments . However, this growth has mainly benefited urban 
areas and industry -related communities, leaving challenges in achieving equitable development 
and environmental protectio n in more rural areas . Despite the economic expansion, Papua New 
Guinea  has faced fiscal difficulties, including rising public debt, downgraded credit ratings, and 
declining foreign exchange reserves, leading to reduced government revenues and economic instability. These issues have limited the government ’s ability to channel further invest ments 
into the forest sectors.  
2.5 Country ownership                          Scale: High  
20. Papua New Guinea  has emerged as a global leader in REDD+ showcasing a strong 
commitment to combating climate change. This effort is central to its n ationally determined 
contributions  (NDCs)  and sustainable development goals, supported by the comprehensive NRS, 
which was developed through extensive consultations.  
21. The Climate Change and Development Authority led the NRS development, engaging 
diverse stakeholders through consultations and workshops. Coordination was  facilitated by the 
National Climate Change Board, the National REDD+ Steering Committee and the technical 
working committees, ensuring a unified approach to climate action.   
2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness              Scale: High  
22. The proceeds will be strategically invested to support  the forest sector and increase 
sustainability of agriculture thereby reducing the pressure on forests. The project aims to


---
*Page 111*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 111 
 
enhance carbon accounting and safeguard systems, enabling Papua New Guinea to access 
broader carbon and climate finance  in the future . Key elements include:  
(a) Rewarding emission reduction success: Provinces with strong performance in reducing 
greenhouse gas emissions are prioritized to reinforce their sustainable practices and 
serve as models for other regions ; 
(b) Recognizing forest values beyond carbon: Funding considers the forest landscape 
integrity index , prioritizing provinces with intact ecosystems to support areas of 
significant ecological and socioeconomic value  and recognizing biodiversity and cultural 
importance ; 
(c) Addressing high d eforestation risks: Provinces with higher population densities and 
greater deforestation risks are prioritized for funding to implement measures against 
logging, agriculture expansion and infrastructure development, thereby promoting 
sustainable land management ; 
(d) Promoting synergies with e xisting initiatives: The selection process evaluates current 
REDD+ and forest conservation program mes to ensure collaboration, avoid duplication 
and enhance the impact of funding by complementing existing efforts.  
III. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
3.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
23. The proposal includes an environmental and s ocial assessment (ESA) that retroactively 
examines the policies and measures (PAMs) implemented by Papua New Guinea during the 
2014 –2016 results period. These PAMs primarily included enabling activities, institutional 
reforms, and policy development supported through REDD+ readiness processes under the Forest Carbon Partner ship Facility and the United Nations Collaborative Programme on 
Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation in Developing Coun tries  (UN-
REDD) . The ESA categorizes PAMs into three groups : readiness, enabling, and direct measures . 
It further assesses four illustrative PAMs
1 selected for their policy significance and relevance to 
forest governance, customary tenure, and land use regulation  
24. Recognizing that ERs during the assessed period are not attributable to discrete physical 
interventions , the ESA adopts a policy and process -based approach to assess consistency with 
applicable safeguards.  It provides a structured review of enabling actions against the  FAO’s 
Framework for Environmental and Social Management, GCF’s interim environmental and social 
safeguards , and  the Cancun Safeguards. A retroactive screening checklist and safeguard 
mapping are included as annexes.  
25. The ESA reports general alignment of the REDD+ readiness activities and enabling 
activities with applicable standards and safeguards. However, due to the indirect nature of the 
results and lack of attribution to specific physical interventions, the assessment relies primarily 
on the consistency of national processes and policy frameworks with th e relevant safeguards. 
The ESA  acknowledges key limitations during the results period, including limited 
documentation of safeguard implementation, weak institutional mechanisms for grievance redress, and the absence of fully operational stakeholder engagement protocols . Gender 
considerations and stakeholder consultations were addressed at a strategic level, though 
coverage and quality varied by activity and location.  While some participatory activities were 
 
1 Implementation of the Forest Policy 1991, PNG moving away from Forest Clearance Authority (FCA) to the Forest 
Management Agreement (FMA), Government’s moratorium on the issuing of Special Agriculture and Business 
Leases (SABLs) in 2011 and subsequent suspension of SABL in 2014, and Strengthening the Management 
Effectiveness of the National System of Protected Areas (GEF Project).


---
*Page 112*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 112 
 
undertaken during REDD+ readiness, the depth and inclusiveness of these engagements varied 
across PAMs and regions. 
26. The ESA demonstrates a reasonable level of due diligence for the period assessed, 
consistent with the expectations for REDD+ RBP proposals as outlined in the REDD+ RBP Pilot 
Programme  TOR. While the enabling nature of the activities and the absence of physical 
interventions constrain the ability to retrospectively evaluate safeguards implementation at the 
community level, these limitations are explicitly acknowledged in the ESA. The AE  has 
committed to addressing outstanding gaps, including those related to  stakeholder engagement, 
grievance mechanisms, through safeguards instruments to be developed during the project 
inception phase, including the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP)  and 
associated instruments.  
27. The proposal is accompanied by an e nvironmental and s ocial management framework 
(ESMF) , which provides the policy and procedural basis for managing environmental and social 
risks during  the implementation of activities financed by RBP proceeds.  The ESMF is positioned  
as a strategic and high -level instrument that will guide the development of a comprehensive 
suite of safeguards instruments during the project’s two -year inception phase, including the 
ESMP, Stakeholder Engagement Plan, Benefit -Sharing Plan, an d risk screening tools. These 
instruments are expected to be finalized and disclosed prior to the commencement of any on -
the-ground activities.  
28. The proposal is classified as c ategory B. The ESMF  outlines relevant standards, including  
the GCF e nvironmental and social safeguards  and the FAO’s environmental and social standards . 
Oversight of safeguards implementation will be carried out by FAO in coordination with PNG’s Climate Change and Development Authority, with operational responsibilities situated within a 
Project Management Unit (PMU).  The PMU is expected to include safeguards expertise, and the 
AE has committed to capacity building for subnational implementing entities and local stakeholders as part of safeguards roll- out during inception.  
29. The ESMF outlines procedural commitments  for screening, risk categorization, 
stakeholder engagement, grievance redress,  information disclosure,  the application of free, 
prior and informed consent,  and gender -responsive planning.  It also describes the development 
of a project -level grievance redress mechanism (GRM), with a multi -tiered structure that 
includes escalation to the FAO Office of the Inspector General. The GRM will include culturally appropriate and survivor -centered procedures to address Sexual E xploitation, Abuse, and 
Harassment (SEAH), supported by awareness -raising activities and targeted communication 
campaigns.  The ESMF commits to transparent disclosure and public access to safeguards 
information, including through workshops and dissemination of materials in appropriate 
formats and languages. The ESMP and associated instruments are expected to incorporate 
monitor ing indicators to track safeguards implementation.   However, since specific 
interventions and sites have not yet been finalized,  the implementation of safeguards remains 
contingent on the effectiveness of measures yet to be operationalized.  
30. The ESMF is consistent with the GCF’s TOR for REDD+ RBP proposals, particularly 
regarding the expectation of proactive ly applying  safeguards for use of proceeds.  It includes a  
non- eligible  list that precludes high -risk activities and specifies that activities involving 
involuntary resettlement, significant adverse impacts on critical habitats, or unresolved legacy 
risks will not be eligible. A fit -for-purpose risk screening checklist will b e finalized during the 
inception phase and used to assess relevant on the ground activities . The i mplementation of 
these safeguards will require close monitoring during the project inception and delivery phases.  
31. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESS7 (Indigenous Peoples) The FP meets the 
requirements of the Indigenous Peoples Policy . The AE has developed a Customary Landholders 
and Communities Planning Framework (CLC PF) as part of the ESMP. Consultations of 
Indigenous Peoples were undertaken during the ex -ante stage though detailed and 
disaggregated information of the involvement of Indigenous Peoples was not made available. At


---
*Page 113*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 113 
 
the ex -poste phase, consultations to Indigenous Peoples are planned to be undertaken prior to 
the implementation of any activity to secure FPIC whenever activities are implemented in 
Indigenous Peoples lands and territories. A benefit -sharing plan will be undertaken in the 
framework of the CLPF, and participatory approaches are also foreseen to encourage a strong 
engagement of Indigenous Peoples in the project. For the ex -poste stage, the AE has been 
requested to provide clear and sufficient information on Indigenous Peoples that will be involved and impacted by the project, share details on the consultations that will be undertaken 
prior to the implementation of any activity, and provide evidence of the FPIC granted by the 
Indigenous Peoples as a condition for implementation. The AE has also been informed of the 
need to share the Indigenous Peoples Plans that will be developed to implement the CLPF when 
specific project areas will be chosen. In line with the IP Policy, the AE has excluded undertaking 
activit ies involving Indigenous Peoples in Voluntary Isolation and First Contact. GCF has highly 
recommended the AE to include a consultant with expert ise on Indigenous Peoples issues in the 
team for the implementation phase. In line with their roles and functions, the Indigenous 
Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and executing entities. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available 
for assi stance at any stage .  
3.2 Gender policy  
32. The AE has submitted a gender assessment and a gender action plan  (GAP)  in 
accordance with the GCF Gender Policy and the REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme TOR. The gender 
assessment presents a detailed review of gender inequalities in P apua New Guinea, highlighting 
persistent gaps in education, political participation, labour force inclusion, and access to land 
and resources. It also identifies elevated levels of gender -based violence and limited 
institutional support for survivors. Within the forestry and climate sectors, the assessment notes that women rely heavily on forests for subsistence needs but have limited decision -
making power or access to benefits, with cultural norms further restricting participation.  
33. The gender assessment presents an overview of gender roles in forestry and REDD+ 
actions in Papua New Guinea, noting that while women rely heavily on forests for subsistence 
needs such as firewood, food, and medicinal plants, they have limited control over high -value 
resources like timber. The assessment notes that women’s participation in REDD+ stakeholder 
consultations has improved over time, yet decision -making remains male -dominated due to 
persistent cultural norms and limited access to education. It al so highlights that gender 
considerations in benefit -sharing mechanisms are limited , with women’s contributions to 
resource management often underrepresented  and sex-disaggregated data remained limited . 
Although PNG’s REDD+ safeguards recognize gender equality and the right to Free, Prior, and 
Informed Consent (FPIC), the assessment identifies implementation gaps, particularly in 
addressing risks related to Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH).  Stakeholder 
consultations are reported to have inco rporated gender considerations, but disaggregated 
participation data and feedback from women stakeholders are not detailed . These constraints 
are consistent with broader limitations noted in the ESA, including evolving processes for safeguards documentation and stakeholder consultation.  
34. The GAP identifies strategic areas of intervention  to support the mainstreaming of 
gender equality during the implementation of activities funded through the use of proceeds. The proposed project includes two components: institutional coordination (Component 1) and 
support for sustainable livelihoods amon g customary landowners (Component 2). Both 
components have potential for gender -responsive delivery.  The GAP proposes strategic actions 
to promote inclusive decision -making, build women's capacity in forest  governance and 
sustainable land use, and support women’s access to climate -resilient livelihoods. Specific 
actions include enhancing women’s participation in land use planning, supporting women -led


---
*Page 114*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 114 
 
enterprises, promoting financial inclusion, and training women in sustainable forestry and 
agricultural practices.  
35. While the GAP outlines key procedural commitments but does not yet include detailed 
measurable outputs, sex -disaggregated indicators, time -bound targets, or a dedicated 
implementation budget. These elements are expected to be further developed during the 
project’s inception phase. I mportantly, the AE has committed to establishing a dedicated 
safeguards team within the Project Management Unit, which will include a Gender Specialist 
alongside environmental and social safeguards experts . The Secretariat suppor ts the AE’s 
commitment to refining the action plan during inception and emphasizes the importance of 
integrating gender -responsive indicators and clear responsibilities into the E SMP , and of 
ensuring that inclusive benefit -sharing mechanisms are developed in parallel during project 
implementation . 
3.3 Risk  
3.3.1.  Accredited entity/executing entity capability  
36. The Government  of Papua New Guinea, through  the Ministry of Environment, 
Conservation and Climate Change, has requested FAO ’s technical and overall assistance for the 
design and implementation of the REDD+ RBP funding proposal. The Government of Papua New 
Guinea  also specifically requested that FAO act as the executing entity (EE) for the proje ct. In 
response to the request, FAO will serve both as accredited entity (AE) and  EE. FAO is one of the 
founding members of the UN -REDD programme and  has significant technical experience and 
technical oversight  in REDD+ and REDD+ RBPs , including in previously approved GCF REDD+ 
RBP projects  in Argentina, Chile  and Colombia.  
3.3.2.  Programme -specific execution risks  
37. Risk of double payments : Papua New Guinea is analysing its participation in other 
jurisdictional payments for REDD+ results that are different from the GCF RBP s, and some 
initiatives are registered in the voluntary  carbon markets. The crediting period of the other 
projects started after 2016 , thus preventing any risk of double payments . 
38. Use of proceeds : The proceeds will be strategically invested in activities outlined in the 
NRS to foster a sustainable and successful REDD+ cycle. This cycle focuses on the essential 
interconnectedness of the three REDD+ phases: r eadiness, implementation and RBPs . 
39. Land tenure : The Department of Lands & Physical Planning  develops and implements 
national policies related to land use, land tenure, and urban development. FAO provides a 
comprehensive set of tools, guidelines, and software systems to support land tenure governance, facilitating improved institutional coordination capacities at national and 
provincial levels in Papua New Guinea  
40. Disbursement plan : GCF will disburse the GCF p roceeds to the A E in a single 
disbursement.  
3.3.3.  Compliance risk ( medium risk)   
41. The funding proposal will be implemented exclusively by FAO, acting as both AE and EE, 
in Papua New Guinea. FAO has assessed the risks of money laundering, terrorist financing, and 
prohibited practices as low in both probability and impact. Nevertheless, as with all p rojects 
operating in fragile governance environments and involving direct execution by the AE, there 
remain inherent risks related to potential misuse of funds, institutional capacity constraints, 
and challenges in ensuring consistent implementation of saf eguards at the field level.


---
*Page 115*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add.15 
Page 115 
 
42. To address these risks, FAO has established internal policies, institutional safeguards, 
and contractual obligations that apply across the project lifecycle. These include anti -money -
laundering and countering the financing of terrorism and prohibited pract ices clauses in the 
transfer agreement with the Government of Papua New Guinea, alignment with multiple FAO 
administrative circulars and sanction policies, and the use of United Nations -wide ineligibility 
and due diligence tools. FAO’s procurement and asset management procedures ensure clear lines of accountability, while the presence of a field -level grievance mechanism – complemented 
by whistleblower protections and awareness campaigns – further enhances transparency and 
responsiveness. Oversight is reinf orced through regular supervision from FAO’s country, 
regional, and headquarters offices.  
43. Considering the project’s operating context, the centralized implementation model, clear 
articulation of risk mitigation responsibilities, and the project’s alignment with established United Nations and FAO standards, the residual compliance risk is assessed as medium. This 
reflects the need for continued oversight and institutional vigilance, and applying the mitigation 
measures embedded in both project design and FAO’s operational framework.   
3.3.4.  GCF portfolio concentration risk   
44. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF concentration risk remains 
within the monitoring thresholds of the Risk Appetite Statement in terms of results areas, single 
proposal or AE concentration.  
Summary risk assessment  
Compliance   Medium  
GCF portfolio concentration   Within the monitoring thresholds  
3.4 Results monitoring and reporting  
45. The reporting of the project will be undertaken following a simplified annual 
performance report for which a specific template has been developed by the Secretariat. The 
frequency of reporting will be annually following the accreditation master agreement ( AMA) .  
(a) The monitoring of the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level according to the description provided in the funding proposal ; 
(b) It should be noted that the funding proposal  review has been undertaken as per d ecision 
B.39/1 3, which called for the REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme TO R, approved at B .18, to be 
followed for this project. In view of that, the requirements under the Evaluation Policy 
for the GCF and the Integrated Results Management Framework  do not apply to the 
project. 
3.5 Legal  assessment  
46. The AMA was signed with the  AE on 8 June 2018 and it became effective on 4 October 
2018. The A E’s five -year accreditation term lapsed on 3 October 2023 but was extended by 
decision B.37/18, paragraph (q), until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the 
five-year accreditation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted 
by the Board. The Board approved the re -accreditation of the A E pursuant to decision B.37/18, 
and the negotiation of the a mended and restated accredi tation master agreement is ongoing . 
47. The A E has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals 
and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project .

BUR1: The annex to the 2017 technical assessment report ( TAR ) (dated 2018) states that “the 
methods used for the FREL/ FRL are not consistent with the latest GHG inventory” and that

inventory in the future” ( see FCCC/TAR/2017/P NG, page 14). Furthermore, the 2017 TAR 
highlights that the definition  of ‘forest’  used in the FREL/ FRL differs from the one used in the 
national GHG inventory and for reporting to the FAO . 
 
BUR2: Annex I to  TATR.2 (2022) states that methods, definitions and information used for the 
assessed FREL/ FRL are overall consistent with those used for the results. However, a different 
post -deforestation removal correction was applied to avoid an overestimation of the results, 
leading to inconsistency with the assessed FREL/ FRL (see para. 16). In para g raph  18, the 
technical annex acknowledges the “ description of how the FREL/ FRL and the estimated results 
could be recalculated in the context of a future submission to the GCF under the REDD+RBP” and 
noted that this is beyond the scope of the technical annex .

1 The TAR (2017) highlighted some issues related to consistency over the time series used for the 
FREL/FRL and the GHG inventory. Papua New Guinea clarified that this should be improved in 
the BUR planned for 2018 at the time of the TAR. The TAR also notes that the use of interpreters 
of the  Collect Earth  tool used different types of satellite imagery, which could lead to time -series 
inconsistencies over the historical reference period and issues related to the identification of 
forest areas subject to disturbances other than logging that could be better i dentified with 
higher -resolution images, thus creating inconsistency over time. Papua  New Guinea explained its 
efforts in securing calibration and quality control and the AT commended Papua New Guinea for 
its efforts. Thus, the issues were considered as re solved.  
(vii) Is the FREL/FRL accurate? (The data and 
methodologies used neither over - nor under-
estimate emissions and/or removals during the 
reference period, so far as can be judged.)  
1 Although the AT of the TAR (2017) recognizes the  country’s  efforts in improving the accuracy of 
the activity data, the report identifies the following issues regarding accuracy of the FREL/FRL:  
- Emissions from forest degradation account for 74 –95% of the annual emission 
estimates used to construct the FREL/ FRL. Although Papua New Guinea used 
the best available data, the AT notes considerable uncertainties around the EFs  
and the methods employed (see paras.  19–22); 
- The report notes that the linear regression model selected is simple and 
transparent, but because it is based on only 13 observations , it may have 
significant statistical errors, leading to low accuracy in the overall results even 
if the accuracy of the measurements in each point is high. However, as the 
FREL/ FRL was recalculated for the GCF submission, this issue does not affect 
the recalculated FREL/ FRL ; 
- About 63% of the plots used for the development of activity data from Collect 
Earth were found to have errors. Papua New Guinea used a  quality 
assurance/quality control  procedure to check and correct these plots and 
managed to address over half of the errors. The TAR commended Papua New 
Guinea  for these efforts , but noted that the remaining errors left significant 
room for increasing the accuracy of the FREL/FRL in future revisions ; 
- Another potential source of inaccuracy was the underestimation of GHG 
removals in the reference period due to the lack of incorporation of rapid 
regrowth in harvested or disturbed tropical forests into the development of EFs  
for forest degradation. Also, the assumption that a degradation event happens 
only once in a particular area of forest, and that all types of disturbance cause 
the same change in biomass/degradation as logging. These may lead to either 
underestimation or overestimation of emissions based on the specific 
circumstances ;


---
*Page 122*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add.15 
Page 122 
 
- The report includes the following recommendations aimed at improving 
accuracy of the FREL/ FRL in the future:  
o Use crop -specific post- deforestation biomass growth rates for non -
forest uses  to increase accuracy ; 
o Improve the determination of forest regrowth in the EF  for 
degradation ; 
o Improve accuracy in the  determination of the losses of biomass carbon 
stocks resulting from different types of disturbances ;  
o Tracking of lands subject to degradation and the inclusion of emissions 
from degradation events subsequent to the first occurrence of 
degradation in the estimation of emissions from the degradation 
activity would further increase the accuracy of the FREL/FR L;  
o Consider including emissions from deadwood. Excluding deadwood 
can lead to a lower accuracy of the FREL/ FRL. 
 
The AT also noted that the ongoing implementation of a complete National Forest Inventory 
(NFI) will lead to information which will help to address these sources of uncertainty when the 
next biennial transparency report is submitted.  
(viii) Have all REDD -plus activities that are 
significant sources of emissions been included?  
2 The 2017 TAR notes that Papua New Guinea  includes reducing emissions from deforestation, 
reducing emissions from forest degradation , and enhancement of forest carbon stocks. The 
emissions and removals from enhancement of forest carbon stock are reported as zero during 
the historical reference period.  
The AT concludes that Papua New Guinea included the three most significant activities.  
  
(ix) Have all of the most significant pools been 
included?  
1 TAR (2017):  
- The FRL includes both living pools: above -ground biomass  and below -ground 
biomass . The TA concluded that the most significant pools in terms of emissions 
from forests were included in the FRL ( see para. 39  of the TAR ). However, 
improvements leading to the inclusion of  SOC  and deadwood were 
recommended; 
- Deadwood is not included and may be significant, because 85 % of the 
population in Papua New Guinea utilizes fuelwood with very high consumption 
levels . Consumption is expected to increase , hence there is significant scope for


---
*Page 123*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add.15 
Page 123 
 
anthropogenic factors to affect this pool. Furthermore, according to  studies 
deadwood in Papua New Guinea may account for 10 –25% of total carbon stocks 
in primary forest and logged forest, respectively , according to field observation. 
In the modified FREL/FRL , Papua New Guinea explains that deadwood was 
excluded owing to a lack of reliable data or default values in the 2006 IPCC 
Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories . Country -specific data 
on deadwood will be available in the future through the ongoing NFI ;  
- Paragraph  32: Regarding SOC , Papua New Guinea confirmed that potentially 
significant emissions may  occur from the loss of carbon in soils owing to 
deforestation (e.g. 20 –40 per cent of total carbon stocks during the conversion 
of forest land to cropland). In its modified FREL/ FRL submission, Papua New 
Guinea explained that, even though the 2006 IPCC Guidelines provide methods 
for estimating CO 2 emissions from SOC , it is currently not possible to estimate 
emissions from the soil carbon pool using the default values provided in the 
2006 IPCC Guidelines because the forest soils in Papua New Guinea have not 
been classified into the soil types provided in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines. The 
country  also  has the intention to include data on SOC obtained from the NFI in 
the revision of the FREL/FRL.  
  
(x) Have all gases that are a significant source of 
emissions been included?  
1 The FREL/ FRL includes CO 2 and exclude s non-CO 2 gases from biomass burning. According to t he 
2017  TAR , considering th at Papua New Guinea reported  that 3.1% of all forests were affected by 
fire and that during El Niño years , “large spikes” in levels of burning occur , non- CO 2 gases  are 
likely to be a significant source of emissions . Furthermore, the FREL/FRL  provided information 
on fire hotspots and location of fires for the period  2002 –2014 , and the modified FREL/FRL 
submission predicts that fires are likely to increase owing to the trends in population and 
shifting agriculture  and noted that non -CO 2 emissions were included in Papua New Guinea’s 
second national communication . The TAR states that “a s p art of the stepwise approach the AT 
considers that Papua New Guinea may wish to monitor and potentially include the non- CO 2 
emissions from biomass burning in future FREL/FRL submissions  (para. 33). ”

Guidelines in the six Land -use categories for constructing the FREL/ FRL (para. 9),  and that the

(xiii) What is the reference period for the 
FREL/FRL?  
1 In the modified FREL/ FRL  assessed by the UNFCCC TA , the historical reference period is 2001 –
2013 (13 years). The FREL/ FRL extrapolates values using a linear regression model for the 
period 2014 –2018.  
The FREL/ FRL was recalculated to align with the GCF  RBP s scorecard , and this recalculation was 
included in the BUR2 submission. The recalculation of the FREL/ FRL was done using the average 
GHG emissions from the period 2009 to 2013 ( five years) . The recalculated FREL/FRL presented 
in BUR2 was applied retrospectively to the results period covered in BUR1 (2014 –201 5), 
therefore the five -year reference period applies to the all results submitted to GCF (2014 –2016) , 
which, when applied to the GCF  RBP scorecard, yields a score of 1.

(xv) Has the country provided information on 
aggregate uncertainties, taking into account  
national capabilities and circumstances?  
2 Section 10 of Papua New Guinea ’s modified FREL/FRL submission (2017) includes an 
uncertainty analysis, which includes  a qualitative and a quantitative uncertainty analysis as well 
as an approach towards reducing errors . However, the FREL/ FRL does not discuss aggregated 
uncertainties as such . 
 
Chapter 6 of the BUR 2 includes  an uncertainty analysis which covers qualitative uncertainty, 
quantitative uncertainty, uncertainty of the EFs and  aggregated uncertainty analys es for both the 
FREL/ FRL 2001 –2013 and the FREL/ FRL 2009 –2013  (recalculated for the purposes of the GCF  
RBP submission),  and for both  the 2014 –2015 and 2016 –2018 REDD+ results period s. The 
results for a 95% confidence interval are as follows:  
- Uncertainty FREL/ FRL (2001 –2013): 10.20 % 
- Uncertainty FREL/ FRL (2009 –2013): 11.17 %. 
The calculation of aggregated uncertainties was prepared using the propagation of error 
approach. For the FREL/ FRL specific to the proposal to GCF (2009 –2013) , the total uncertainty 
for deforestation is 25.58% and  for degradation is  13.19%. 
All uncertainties are below 30%, hence, according to the scorecard, a score of 2 is given.

(i) Are the reported results in the technical annex 
to the BUR consistent with the FREL/FRL 
(including the same pools, activities and gases)?  2 TATR.1 (2019)  explains that Papua New Guinea  en sured overall consistency between its 
FREL/ FRL and its estimation of the results of the implementation of the activities reducing


---
*Page 126*

GCF/B. 42/02/Add.15 
Page 126 
 
emissions from deforestation, reducing emissions from forest degradation and enhancement of 
forest carbon stocks in 2014 –2015 by:  
- Using consistent methodologies and data to generate activity data ;  
- Using consistent methodologies and data to generate EFs for both FREL/FRL 
and results ; 
- Including the same carbon pools ; 
- Including the same GHG (CO 2); 
- Using the same forest definition . 
In para. 16 of the same report , the land use, land -use change and forestry  (LULUCF ) experts 
concluded that the results presented o n the implementation of the activities reducing emissions 
from deforestation, reducing emissions from forest degradation and enhancement of forest 
carbon stocks are largely consistent with the assessed FREL/ FRL. Enhancement is not included 
as a separate activity , but is accounted for in a correction for regrowth in sites subject to 
deforestation.   
 
The LULUCF experts noted that the consistency between the FREL/ FRL and the GHG inventory 
included in Papua New Guinea’s  first BUR had increased, most notably owing to its use of the  
Collect Earth  tool to estimate a ctivity data for both the technical annex and LULUCF. However, 
some inconsistencies with the GHG inventory remain.  
 
In TATR.2 (2022) , para. 44 concludes that the LULUCF experts consider the data and 
information provided in the technical annex to be transparent  and overall consistent, complete 
and accurate. Annex II  states that “methods, definitions and information used for the assessed 
FREL/ FRL are overall consistent with those used for the results”. However, a different post-
deforestation removal correction factor was applied  du e to Papua New Guinea’s realization that 
the approach in the FREL/FRL did not reflect the assumed increase in deforestation over time 
during the results period, and this correction factor was therefore required  t o avoid an 
overestimation of the results . In effect, therefore, there is inconsistency between the FREL/FRL 
and reported results, but this inconsistency is a result of improvements in the methodology over 
time. Hence this is not a justification for a ‘fail’ . The consistency between the approaches in the 
two BURs was commended by the TAR, wh ich also concluded that this difference between 
FREL/FRL and results measurement did not affect overall consistency between the two.

(ii) Are the data and information provided in the 
technical annex transparent? (Has information 
been provided to allow an understanding of how 
UNFCCC guidance on results reporting has been 
addressed?) 
1 In TATR.1 (2019) , paragraph  23, the LULUCF experts concluded that Papua New Guinea  
provided the necessary information to facilitate their understanding of the estimation of the 
results of the implementation of REDD+ activities. The data and information provided in the 
technical annex are overall considered to be transparent, consistent, complete and accurate to 
the extent possible. In paragraph  32, the experts noted that Papua New Guinea’s estimation of 
the results of the implementation of the REDD+ activities in Papua New Guinea was undertaken 
using, overall, a transparent and consisten t approach.  
 
However, t he LULUCF experts also noted  that Papua New Guinea’s  technical annex and 
supplementary information did not provide a detailed description of the statistical procedure for 
estimating activity data  from the Collect Earth  sample plots, or the raw data used for estimating 
activity data  (see para. 19  of TATR.1 ). According to the report, this negatively  affects  both 
transparency and completeness.   
 
Furthermore, the TATR.2 explains that although Papua New Guinea provide s an additional 
calculation spreadsheet , it was not possible to reconstruct the results.  
   
(iii) Are the data and information provided in the 
technical annex complete? (Has information been 
provided that allows for the reconstruction of the 
results?)  
1 TATR.1 (2019) , paragraph  23, states that t he data and information provided in the technical 
annex are overall considered to be transparent, consistent, complete and accurate to the extent 
possible.  
However, the absence of a clear description of the statistical procedure used to estimate activity 
data  on the basis of the sample plots and of the raw data used to generate activity data  meant it 
was not possible to fully reconstruct the results. The LULUCF experts noted this as an area for 
technical improvement that would increase the transparency and completeness of future 
submissions.  
 
TATR.2 (2022) , paragraph  19, explains that although Papua New Guinea provided additional 
information and the calculation spreadsheet, it was not possible to fully reconstruct the results 
due to “the absence of a clear description of the statistical procedure used to estimate [activity 
data]  from the sampling plots, and of the raw data used to generate [activity data]” (p age 6).

GCF/B. 42/02/Add.15

It was also the case that judgment of whether a change was due to anthropogenic or non -
anthropogenic factors was based on the local knowledge and experience of the officer analy sing 
the data, without which the reconstruction of results is not possible, hence the TATR’s 
recommendation that Papua New Guinea develop clearer objective guidelines to govern the 
process in future . 
 
(iv) Are the data and information provided in the 
technical annex consistent? (Were data and 
methodologies applied consistently over the 
results time series?)  
 
1 TATR.1 (2019) , paragraph 23, states that t he data and information provided in the technical 
annex are overall considered to be transparent, consist ent, complete and accurate to the extent 
possible .  
The summary states that the described methods, definitions  and information used for reporting 
the assessed FREL/ FRL and results are overall consistent. A post- deforestation removal 
correction factor was only applied for the results period and not for the reference period. 
Paragraph  15 of TATR.1  explains that Papua New Guinea introduced a new methodology for the 
treatment of post- deforestation regrowth in order to avoid systematic overestimation of the 
results , and  the assessed FREL/ FRL did not include the exponential curve described in the 
technical annex  related to post- deforestation removals, thus causing an inconsistency between 
the assessed FREL/ FRL and the technical annex. Papua New Guinea may include the proposed 
update in future FREL/FRLs.  However, this inconsistency was the result of an improvement in 
the methodology over time and should not constitute a rationale for a lower mark on the 
scorecard.  
 
The comments in TATR.2 (2022) are almost the same as in TATR.1.  Paragraph  17 states that the 
LULUCF experts concluded that the results presented from  implementing the activities reducing 
emissions from deforestation, reducing emissions from forest degradation and enhancement of 
forest carbon stocks are overall consistent with the assessed FREL/ FRL.  
However, as indicated in para graph  20 of TATR.2 , the report states that Papua New Guinea did 
not maintain consistency in terms of sources of activity data  and EFs with those used in the GHG 
inventory included in the second communication or for the estimated results of implementing 
activities reducing emissions from the three activities included. This  is important because , 
according to decision 12/CP.17, para graph  8, “the FREL/ FRL shall be established taking into 
account decision 4/CP.15, paragraph 7, and maintaining consistency with the anthropogenic 
forest- related GHG emissions by sources and removals by sinks reported in the Party’s GHG 
inventory .” On this basis, a score of 1 is applied.

(v) Are the data and information provided in the 
technical annex accurate? (Does the annex neither 
over - nor under- estimate emissions and/or 
removals?)  
1 TATR.1 (2019)  identifies several issues that can reduce accuracy . Many of these issues were 
linked to the incomplete nature of the NFI, which was still under implementation at the time : 
- Lack of country -specific post -deforestation biomass growth rates for non -forest land 
uses;  
- In tropical regions, biomass may accumulate quickly, thus making it difficult to 
determine the extent to which the EFs include forest regrowth, because plots may have 
been established from between a few months to up to four years after logging. This 
approach  may result in a significant level of uncertainty and cause an underestimation 
of CO 2 removals during the historical reference period . The TATR.1 identifies the 
accurate determination of the extent of forest regrowth included in the EF for 
degradation as  an area for future technical improvement ;  
- The use of a higher carbon fraction of dry matter for determining above -ground biomass 
as the carbon fraction provided in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines ( see table 4.3) ; 
- Using the same loss of biomass for all types of disturbance types when determining the 
EF for forest degradation (34.5% loss as used for commercial logging). The LULUCF 
experts noted that this assumes that all types of disturbance affect forest carbon stocks 
in the same way as commercial logging. They also noted that this assumption may 
reduce the accuracy of the estimated emissions from forest degradation ;  
- The calculation of EFs for degradation assumed that a degradation event happens only 
once in a particular area of forest, thus underestimating emissions due to successive 
disturbances ;  
- The team assessing the FREL/FRL noted that all forests subject to gardening were 
assumed to be primary forests, and also noted that these regrown forest patches are not 
likely to contain biomass carbon stocks comparable to primary forests. This assumption  
may lead to overestimation of the emissions from forest degradation.  
TATR.2 (2022)  states that  th e information in the BUR2  uses more recent and accurate 
information.  In para. 27  of TATR.2,  the AT states that “the data and information provided in the 
technical annex are considered to be transparent, overall consistent, complete and accurate to 
the extent possible”. The completion of the NFI allowed for many of the issues identified in 
TATR.1 to be addressed. The TA identifies checking the accuracy of units and clarifying the data 
sets used for the land -use change assessment as an area for future improvement related to 
accuracy .

GCF/B. 42/02/Add.15 
Page 130

 Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment  of 
FP273 
Proposal name:  Papua New Guinea REDD -plus RBP for results period 2014 -  
2016  
Accredited entity:  Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)  
Country:  Papua New Guinea  
Project size:  Medium  
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 Overview  
1. This is the first submission of the funding proposal  Papua New Guinea  REDD+ RBP  for 
results 2014 – 2016 submitted to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) under the 
Results -Based Payment (RBP) modality. The accredited entity (AE) is the United Nations Food 
and Agriculture Organization (FAO).  
2. In the context of REDD+1, Results - based Payment refers to ex -post payments made for 
climate change mitigation results that a country has achieved  already .  Th ese mitigation results 
are determined by comparing the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and carbon enhancements 
as defined in a country’s Forest Reference Emission Level (FREL) or Forest Reference Level 
(FRL) submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention  on Climate Change (UNFCCC), 
and the actual GHG emissions and carbon enhancement reported in national reports  to the 
UNFCCC  known as Biennial Update Reports (BURs) 2. If the net GHG emissions in the FRL/FREL 
are higher than the net GHG emissions reported in the BURs, the country can claim a results -
based payment.  
3.  The Board decision B.40/1 6 from October 2024 establishes the GCF Policy for results -
based payments for REDD+  (RBP). The policy outlines how GCF incorporates payments for 
verified results of REDD+ activities into its standard project and programme activity cycle. 
According to the policy, such payments can apply to “REDD+ results that have been fully 
measured, rep orted and assessed in accordance with Article 5 of the Paris Agreement, the 
decisions of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+ of the United Nations Frame work Convention 
on Climate Change (UNFCCC)” ( decision B.40/16, objective).  
4. At B.39 the Board agreed to allow, on an exceptional basis, four pending REDD+ RBP 
concept notes submitted to GCF before the closure of the pilot programme in 2022 to be submitted as funding proposal s under the Pilot Programme (decision B.39/13, paras . (d) and 
(e)).  The current funding proposal  from Papua New Guinea is based on one of those concept 
notes. The REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme followed the policy adopted by the Board in decision 
B.18/07, with the accompanying terms of reference in  annex XI to decision  B.18/07. The GCF 
 
1 Reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation and the role of conservation, sustainable 
management of forests and enhancement of forest carbon stocks in developing countries (REDD+) is a framework 
negotiated under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change to facilitate intergovernmental 
cooperation on forests and climate change . Forests are vitally important for achieving the goals of the Paris 
Agreement, and the REDD+ framework is therefore recognized in Article 5 of the Paris Agreement. Detailed rules 
for REDD+ were agreed by the Conference of the Parties  under the Warsaw Framework for REDD+.  
2 See https://unfccc.int/biennial -update- reports .


---
*Page 139*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 139 
 
 
   
 Secretariat has prepared the corresponding templates, which, to some extent, differ from the 
templates to other modalities for GFC funding proposal s. 
5. The assessment of RBP funding proposals comprises two steps. First a backward -
looking assessment is made of the net GHG emission reductions already achieved for which the 
proposal is requesting results -based payments. This is conducted using a scorecard de fined by 
the Board (the scorecard for the REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme is contained in annex XII to 
decision B.18/07). Second, a forward -looking assessment is made of the use of proceeds and the 
expected non -carbon benefits, which is evaluated against the six  GCF investment criteria.  
6. This assessment report follows the same structure. Section 1.1 summarizes the findings 
from the backward -looking assessment using the scorecard. Sections 1.2 onwards present the 
forward -looking assessment of the use of proceeds, organized according to the six investment 
criteria established by GCF.  
1.2 Summary of scorecard results  
Scorecard Section  Results  
Carbon elements  Score: 
35/48  All criteria “pass”  
Non -carbon 
elements  Cancun safeguards:  All criteria “pass”  
Use of proceeds and non -carbon benefits  Score: 2  
7. Based on the  scorecard results and following the terms of reference procedures, the 
independent TAP recommends that the Board consider the following:  
(i) GCF volume of emission reductions: 12,373,415.83 tonnes CO2 equivalent  
(ii) An additional 2.5 per cent for the use of the proceeds and non -carbon benefits  
(iii) Proposed REDD+ RBP (USD 5/t CO 2 eq)3: USD 63,413,756.  
8. The emission reduction results for which Papua New Guinea is asking for results -based 
payment through this funding proposal  refer to the difference between the historical GHG 
emissions during the period 2009 – 20134 and the GHG emissions in the period 2014 – 2016, 
which are lower.  
9. The period 2014 –2016 was selected because it is the period covered by the REDD+ RBP 
GCF Pilot Programme. This time frame spans two separate UNFCCC reporting periods, 2014 –
2015 and 2016 –2018, for which Papua New Guinea reported to the UNFCCC secretariat thr ough 
two Biennial Update Reports (first and second BUR).  The GCF volume of emission reductions 
correspond to the sum of the emission reductions from Papua New Guinea in 2014, 2015 and 
2016 only.  
10. In addition to the funding proposal package, the iTAP considered the following 
documents for the backward -looking assessment of emission reductions:  
(i) “Papua New Guinea’s National REDD+ Forest Reference Level. Modified 
submission for UNFCCC Technical Assessment in 2017

11. Main findings on the section on Forest Reference Emission Level/Forest Reference 
Level (FREL/FRL):  Papua New Guinea recalculated its FRL in order to be aligned with GCF  
requirements . However, the technical assessments listed above highlight issues related to 
consistency, transparency of data, completeness, accuracy and the selection of the most 
significant carbon pools and GHGs  included. As a result, the FREL/FRL component received 
some deductions in the scorecard, in line with the Board decision and the co rresponding terms 
of reference for the RBP Pilot Programme (see annex I for further details).  
12. Main findings on REDD+ results reporting. The technical assessments identified 
issues related to the completeness, transparency, consistency and accuracy of the data 
presented in the REDD+ results reports. In addition, concerns were raised regarding the 
treatment of aggregate uncertainties. These shortcomings also led to score deductions under 
the REDD+ results reporting section, following the criteria established by the Board and the relevant terms of reference for the RBP Pilot Programme ( see annex I for further details).  
13. The funding proposal submitted a volume of 16,969,256 tonnes CO 2 equivalent . Due to 
the findings presented in the two paragraphs above, the final score of the carbon elements is 
35/48, therefore the GCF volume of emission reductions is 12,373,415.83  tonnes CO 2 
equivalent . 
1.3 Impact potential                Scale:  High  
14. From this section onwards the assessment adopts a forward -looking perspective and 
focuses on the proposed use of proceeds.  
15. The objective of the use of proceeds is to support the national G overnment and six of the 
provincial governments of Papua New Guinea to implement the National REDD+ Strategy (NRS)  
for 2017 –2027. The emphasis is on the following action areas: strengthened land -use planning, 
law enforcement, protection of forest s and sustainable livelihoods. Achieving this objective 
should contribute to reduc ing emissions from land use, deforestation  and forest degradation ,


---
*Page 141*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 15 
Page 141 
 
 
   
 the sustainable management of forest, and the conservation and enhancement of carbon 
stocks.5 
16. The funding proposal  is structured around two components. Component 1 aims at 
increasing the institutional coordination capacities of national and provincial governments in 
Papua New Guinea , while component 2 seeks to enhance economic productivity and inclusive 
sustainable livelihoods of customary landowners and local communities in the targeted 
provinces.  
17. The funding proposal  covers six provinces: West Sepik, East Sepik, Western Province, 
Hela, Chimbu and West New Britain Province. The selection of these provinces resulted from 
applying the following criteria: i) rewarding provinces with high performance in reducing 
emissions,  ii) recognizing values of forest beyond carbon, based on a forest landscape integrity 
index, and iii) considering provinces that may have a higher risk of deforestation and forest 
degradation because of anthropogenic pressure based on population density, which is 
considered as a major driver  of deforestation and forest degradation  in the whole country.  
18. Component 1 will promote enabling conditions at the provincial level and component 2 
will offer context -specific livelihood options that address the drivers of deforestation and forest 
degradation at the local level, while providing further development outcomes. For more information about the development outcomes, see  section 1.4  below . 
19. While RBP funding proposal s to GCF are not required to include a detailed a nnex 22 
estimating the mitigation impact potential, this proposal provides an initial estimate of 3 –4.5 Mt 
CO₂ eq to be reduced over the six -year implementation period. This figure is based on NRS  
financing needs, estimated at USD 1 billion over 10 years. In addition, the proposal states that 
more than 510,000 people, equivalent to approximately 4.5  per cent  of Papua New Guinea’s 
population , will benefit from the project.  
20. The iTAP assessment is informed by the four elements of Papua New Guinea’s national 
framework to implement REDD+, which is aligned to the corresponding decisions in the 
UNFCCC: the National REDD+ strategy for Papua New Guinea for the period 2017 -2027 (NRS) as 
submitted to the UNFCCC REDD+  Hub6, the National Forest Monitoring System, the revised 
version of the Forest Reference Level, and the Safeguard Information System.  
21. The structure of the funding proposal  demonstrates strong alignment with key action 
areas mentioned in the NRS , including strengthening climate change legislation, financing and 
management , and enhanced economic productivity and sustainable livelihoods. Similarly, the 
proposal clarifies how monitoring will be integrated into the National Forest Monitoring System 
and calculated against the FRL , as well as how the project will contribute to document ing 
REDD+ results through the REDD+ Registry Data Management System.  
22. In terms of reporting results to GCF, the AE will use the simplified Annual Performance 
Report  (APR) , developed by the GCF Secretariat in accord ance  with  decision B.18/07, annex XI , 
section V, footnote 19 . Furthermore, the AE clarified to the iTAP how, in addition to the RBP 
monitoring and the requirements of GCF, FAO will monitor the progress of activities using a project -specific logical framework. The iTAP recommends that, to the extent possible in the 
implementation of the RBP policy, the AE consider facilitating oversight of the funds disbursed to subnational governments and communities, promoting accountability across jurisdictions.  
23. In view of the above, the iTAP assesses the climate impact potential as high.
